### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. Gaccho has an N-sided field and M potatoes
2. He can only manage fields up to the K-th side (fields 1 to K)
3. When planting potatoes in field i:
   - Each planted potato yields `ai` potatoes after one year
   - Maximum of `bi` potatoes can be planted in field i
4. Goal: Maximize total potatoes after one year by optimally distributing M potatoes among fields 1 to K

## Key Insights

1. Since each field i gives a yield of `ai` potatoes per planted potato, we should prioritize fields with higher `ai` values
2. We have a constraint of maximum `bi` potatoes per field
3. We need to distribute M potatoes optimally across fields 1 to K

## Strategy

1. Since we want to maximize yield, we should sort the fields by their yield ratio (ai) in descending order
2. For the first K fields, we should allocate potatoes to the ones with highest ai values first
3. For each field, we can plant at most bi potatoes
4. We need to distribute M potatoes optimally across the top K fields by yield

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read input: N, M, K, a array, b array
2. Consider only fields 1 to K (indices 0 to K-1)
3. Sort these K fields by their yield ratio (ai) in descending order
4. Distribute potatoes greedily: allocate as many as possible to the field with highest ai, then move to next, until all M potatoes are distributed
5. For each field, don't exceed its maximum capacity bi

## Implementation

```python
# Read input
N, M, K = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# We can only use fields 1 to K (0-indexed: 0 to K-1)
# Sort fields by yield ratio (ai) in descending order to maximize yield
fields = []
for i in range(K):
    fields.append((a[i], b[i]))

# Sort by yield (a[i]) in descending order
fields.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)

total_potatoes = 0
remaining_potatoes = M

# Distribute potatoes greedily to fields with highest yield first
for yield_rate, max_plant in fields:
    # How many potatoes can we plant in this field?
    can_plant = min(remaining_potatoes, max_plant)
    total_potatoes += can_plant * yield_rate
    remaining_potatoes -= can_plant
    
    # If no potatoes left, we're done
    if remaining_potatoes == 0:
        break

print(total_potatoes)
```

## Verification with Examples

**Example 1:**
- N=5, M=100, K=3
- a = [2,3,4,5,6], b = [50,40,20,10,5]
- Fields 1-3: a=[2,3,4], b=[50,40,20]
- Sort by yield: (4,20), (3,40), (2,50)
- Field 1 (yield 4): plant min(100,20) = 20 → yield = 20×4 = 80, remaining = 80
- Field 2 (yield 3): plant min(80,40) = 40 → yield = 40×3 = 120, remaining = 40  
- Field 3 (yield 2): plant min(40,50) = 40 → yield = 40×2 = 80
- Total = 80 + 120 + 80 = 280 ✓

**Example 2:**
- N=5, M=100, K=3
- a = [2,3,4,5,100], b = [50,40,20,10,1]

```python
# Read input
N, M, K = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# Only consider fields 1 to K (0-indexed: 0 to K-1)
fields = []
for i in range(K):
    fields.append((a[i], b[i]))

# Sort by yield rate in descending order to maximize total yield
fields.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)

total_yield = 0
remaining_potatoes = M

for yield_rate, max_plant in fields:
    # Plant as many as possible in this field (up to max_plant or remaining potatoes)
    plant_count = min(remaining_potatoes, max_plant)
    total_yield += plant_count * yield_rate
    remaining_potatoes -= plant_count
    
    # If no more potatoes left, break early
    if remaining_potatoes == 0:
        break

print(total_yield)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Problem

Gaccho owns an N-sided field and M potatoes. Each field is numbered from 1 to N. Gaccho wants to increase the number of potatoes by planting and harvesting potatoes in the field.

Gaccho lives alone and can only manage fields up to the K side. In addition, the soil condition and area of ​​each field vary, and the number of potatoes harvested and the number of potatoes that can be planted also vary. When potatoes are planted in field i, ai potatoes can be harvested for each potato planted in field i one year later. However, only bi potatoes can be planted in the field i at the maximum.

When you plant M or less potatoes in a field within K side, please find the maximum number of potatoes you can own one year later.

Constraints

* 1 ≤ N ≤ 15
* 1 ≤ M ≤ 104
* 1 ≤ K ≤ min (N, 3)
* 1 ≤ ai ≤ 103
* 1 ≤ bi ≤ 104

Input

The input is given in the following format.


NMK
a1 a2 ... aN
b1 b2 ... bN


Three integers N, M, K are given on the first line, separated by blanks.
N integers ai are given on the second line, separated by blanks.
N integers bi are given on the third line, separated by blanks.

Output

Output the maximum number of potatoes in one line.

Examples

Input

5 100 3
2 3 4 5 6
50 40 20 10 5


Output

280


Input

5 100 3
2 3 4 5 100
50 40 20 10 1


Output

339

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an N-sided field with M potatoes initially
2. We can only manage fields up to the K-th side (K ≤ min(N, 3))
3. For field i:
   - When we plant `x` potatoes, we get `a_i * x` potatoes harvested after one year
   - Maximum we can plant in field i is `b_i`
4. We want to maximize the total potatoes after one year

## Key Insights

1. We can only use the first K fields (fields 1 to K)
2. We need to distribute our M potatoes among these K fields to maximize the total harvest
3. Since the harvest ratio `a_i` is linear, we should prioritize fields with higher `a_i` values
4. This is essentially a greedy allocation problem where we should allocate more potatoes to fields with higher yield ratios

## Strategy

1. Among the first K fields, sort them by their `a_i` values in descending order (highest yield first)
2. Allocate our M potatoes optimally to maximize the total harvest
3. Since we can plant at most `b_i` potatoes in field i, we need to respect these constraints

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read input values
2. Extract the first K fields (indices 0 to K-1)
3. Sort these fields by their yield ratio `a_i` in descending order
4. Greedily allocate potatoes to fields with highest a_i first
5. For each field, allocate up to its maximum capacity b_i, and remaining potatoes go to the next field

## Implementation

```python
# Read input
N, M, K = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# We can only use fields 1 to K (0-indexed: 0 to K-1)
# Among these, we want to use fields with highest a_i first

# Create list of fields with their a_i and b_i values
fields = []
for i in range(K):
    fields.append((a[i], b[i]))

# Sort by yield ratio (a_i) in descending order
fields.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)

# Greedily allocate potatoes
total_harvest = 0
remaining_potatoes = M

for yield_ratio, max_plant in fields:
    # How many potatoes can we plant in this field?
    plant_amount = min(remaining_potatoes, max_plant)
    # Harvest from this field
    total_harvest += yield_ratio * plant_amount
    # Update remaining potatoes
    remaining_potatoes -= plant_amount
    
    # If no potatoes remain, break
    if remaining_potatoes == 0:
        break

print(total_harvest)
```

- Sort by a_i descending: (4,20), (3,40), (2,50)
- Allocate: 20×4 = 80, 40×3 = 120, 40×2 = 80 → total = 80+120+80 = 280 ✓

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have N fields numbered 1 to N, and M potatoes initially
2. We can only manage fields up to K sides (meaning we can only use the first K fields)
3. For field i:
   - If we plant x potatoes, we get `a[i] * x` potatoes harvested after one year
   - Maximum we can plant in field i is `b[i]` potatoes
4. We want to maximize the total harvest after one year

## Key Insights

1. Since we can only use fields 1 to K, we need to decide how to distribute our M potatoes among these K fields
2. For each field i, the return rate is `a[i]` (potatoes harvested per planted potato)
3. To maximize harvest, we should plant more potatoes in fields with higher `a[i]` values
4. We need to consider the constraint that we can't exceed `b[i]` potatoes in field i

## Strategy

1. We have K fields to consider (fields 1 to K)
2. We need to distribute M potatoes among these K fields, with the constraint that field i can take at most `b[i]` potatoes
3. We should prioritize fields with higher `a[i]` values
4. Since we want to maximize the return, we sort the fields by `a[i]` in descending order and plant as much as possible in high-return fields first

## Algorithm

1. Take the first K fields (indices 0 to K-1)
2. Sort them by their return rate `a[i]` in descending order
3. For each field (in order of decreasing return rate):
   - Plant as many potatoes as possible (up to min(remaining potatoes, b[i]))
   - Add the harvest (a[i] * planted amount) to total
4. Return the total harvest

## Step-by-step Solution

Let me trace through the first example:
- N=5, M=100, K=3
- a = [2,3,4,5,6]
- b = [50,40,20,10,5]
- We can use fields 1,2,3 (indices 0,1,2)
- a values: [2,3,4] → sorted descending: [4,3,2]
- Field 2 (a=4, b=20): plant min(100,20) = 20 → harvest = 4*20 = 80
- Field 1 (a=3, b=40): plant min(80,40) = 40 → harvest = 3*40 = 120  
- Field 3 (a=2, b=50): plant min(40,50) = 40 → harvest = 2*40 = 80
- Total = 80 + 120 + 80 = 280 ✓

```python
# Read input
N, M, K = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# We can only use fields 1 to K (indices 0 to K-1)
fields = []
for i in range(K):
    fields.append((a[i], b[i]))

# Sort by return rate (a[i]) in descending order
fields.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)

# Distribute potatoes optimally
total_harvest = 0
remaining_potatoes = M

for return_rate, max_planting in fields:
    to_plant = min(remaining_potatoes, max_planting)
    total_harvest += return_rate * to_plant
    remaining_potatoes -= to_plant

print(total_harvest)
```

### Answer: (use the provided format with backticks)

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an N-sided field with M potatoes initially
2. We can only manage fields from 1 to K (first K fields)
3. When we plant potatoes in field i:
   - We get `ai` potatoes harvested for each potato planted (one year later)
   - Maximum we can plant in field i is `bi` potatoes
4. Goal: Find maximum number of potatoes after one year

## Key Insights

1. Since we can only use fields 1 to K, we should consider only these K fields
2. For each field i (1 to K), if we plant `x` potatoes, we get `x * ai` potatoes harvested
3. We have a total of M potatoes to distribute across fields 1 to K
4. We want to maximize the total harvest: sum of `x_i * a_i` across all fields
5. For field i, we're limited by `x_i ≤ b_i` and `x_i ≥ 0`
6. The total planted potatoes must be ≤ M: sum of `x_i` ≤ M

## Strategy

Since we want to maximize the harvest, we should prioritize fields with higher `a_i` values (higher yield per potato planted).

This is a greedy approach:
1. Among the first K fields, sort them by their yield `a_i` in descending order
2. Distribute all M potatoes to the fields with the highest yields first
3. For each field, we can't exceed its planting limit `b_i`

## Algorithm Steps

1. Extract the first K fields (indices 0 to K-1)
2. Create a list of fields with their yield `a_i` and planting limit `b_i`
3. Sort this list by yield in descending order
4. Greedily distribute potatoes to the highest yielding fields first
5. For each field, plant as many potatoes as possible (up to its limit `b_i`) or until we run out of total potatoes

## Implementation

```python
# Read input
N, M, K = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# We can only use fields 1 to K (indices 0 to K-1)
fields = []
for i in range(K):
    fields.append((a[i], b[i]))  # (yield, limit)

# Sort fields by yield in descending order to prioritize high-yield fields
fields.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)

# Greedily distribute potatoes to fields with highest yields first
total_harvest = 0
remaining_potatoes = M

for yield_val, limit in fields:
    # Plant as many potatoes as possible in this field
    to_plant = min(remaining_potatoes, limit)
    total_harvest += to_plant * yield_val
    remaining_potatoes -= to_plant
    
    # If no more potatoes left, break
    if remaining_potatoes == 0:
        break

print(total_harvest)
```

- N=5, M=100, K=3
- a = [2,3,4,5,6]
- b = [50,40,20,10,5]
- Fields 1-3: [(2,50), (3,40), (4,20)]
- Sort by yield: [(4,20), (3,40), (2,50)]
- Field 1 (yield=4): plant min(100,20)=20 → harvest = 20*4 = 80, remaining = 80
- Field 2 (yield=3): plant min(80,40)=40 → harvest = 40*3 = 120, remaining = 40  
- Field 3 (yield=2): plant min(40,50)=40 → harvest = 40*2 = 80
- Total = 80+120+80 = 280 ✓

**Example 2:**

- Field 2 (yield=3): plant min(80,40)=40 → harvest = 40*3 = 120, remaining = 40

```python
# Read input
N, M, K = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# We can only use fields 1 to K (indices 0 to K-1)
fields = []
for i in range(K):
    fields.append((a[i], b[i]))  # (yield, limit)

# Sort fields by yield in descending order to prioritize high-yield fields
fields.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)

# Greedily distribute potatoes to fields with highest yields first
total_harvest = 0
remaining_potatoes = M

for yield_val, limit in fields:
    # Plant as many potatoes as possible in this field
    to_plant = min(remaining_potatoes, limit)
    total_harvest += to_plant * yield_val
    remaining_potatoes -= to_plant
    
    # If no more potatoes left, break
    if remaining_potatoes == 0:
        break

print(total_harvest)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

Problem

Gaccho owns an N-sided field and M potatoes. Each field is numbered from 1 to N. Gaccho wants to increase the number of potatoes by planting and harvesting potatoes in the field.

Gaccho lives alone and can only manage fields up to the K side. In addition, the soil condition and area of ​​each field vary, and the number of potatoes harvested and the number of potatoes that can be planted also vary. When potatoes are planted in field i, ai potatoes can be harvested for each potato planted in field i one year later. However, only bi potatoes can be planted in the field i at the maximum.

When you plant M or less potatoes in a field within K side, please find the maximum number of potatoes you can own one year later.

Constraints

* 1 ≤ N ≤ 15
* 1 ≤ M ≤ 104
* 1 ≤ K ≤ min (N, 3)
* 1 ≤ ai ≤ 103
* 1 ≤ bi ≤ 104

Input

The input is given in the following format.


NMK
a1 a2 ... aN
b1 b2 ... bN


Three integers N, M, K are given on the first line, separated by blanks.
N integers ai are given on the second line, separated by blanks.
N integers bi are given on the third line, separated by blanks.

Output

Output the maximum number of potatoes in one line.

Examples

Input

5 100 3
2 3 4 5 6
50 40 20 10 5


Output

280


Input

5 100 3
2 3 4 5 100
50 40 20 10 1


Output

339

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have N fields numbered 1 to N, and M potatoes initially
2. We can only manage fields up to K sides (first K fields)
3. For field i:
   - If we plant `x` potatoes, we get `a[i] * x` potatoes harvested next year
   - Maximum we can plant in field i is `b[i]`
4. We want to maximize the total potatoes after one year of harvesting

## Key Insights

1. We can only use fields 1 to K (first K fields)
2. The profit from field i is `a[i]` per planted potato
3. We need to distribute M potatoes across the first K fields to maximize total harvest
4. Since we want to maximize profit, we should prioritize fields with higher `a[i]` values
5. We're constrained by the maximum planting capacity `b[i]` for each field

## Strategy

1. Among the first K fields, we should sort them by profit rate `a[i]` in descending order
2. Distribute our M potatoes starting from the highest profit field to the lowest
3. For each field, plant as many potatoes as possible up to its capacity `b[i]`
4. Continue with remaining potatoes until we've used all M potatoes

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read input values
2. Extract the first K fields (indices 0 to K-1)
3. Sort these fields by `a[i]` in descending order
4. Greedily distribute M potatoes to the fields with highest profit first
5. For each field in sorted order:
   - Plant min(remaining_potatoes, b[i]) potatoes
   - Add a[i] * planted_potatoes to total harvest
   - Subtract planted_potatoes from remaining_potatoes

## Implementation

```python
# Read input
N, M, K = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# We can only use fields 1 to K, which are indices 0 to K-1
# Create list of fields with their (profit_rate, capacity) values
fields = []
for i in range(K):
    fields.append((a[i], b[i]))

# Sort by profit rate in descending order (highest profit first)
fields.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)

# Distribute potatoes greedily to maximize harvest
remaining_potatoes = M
total_harvest = 0

for profit_rate, capacity in fields:
    # Plant as many potatoes as possible in this field
    plant_count = min(remaining_potatoes, capacity)
    total_harvest += profit_rate * plant_count
    remaining_potatoes -= plant_count

print(total_harvest)
```

- Sorted by profit: (4,20), (3,40), (2,50)
- Field 2 (profit 4): plant min(100,20) = 20 potatoes → harvest = 4*20 = 80
- Remaining: 80
- Field 1 (profit 3): plant min(80,40) = 40 potatoes → harvest = 3*40 = 120  
- Remaining: 40
- Field 0 (profit 2): plant min(40,50) = 40 potatoes → harvest = 2*40 = 80
- Total: 80 + 120 + 80 = 280 ✓

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an N-sided field and M potatoes initially
2. We can only manage fields up to the Kth side (fields 1 to K)
3. For field i:
   - If we plant x potatoes, we harvest a[i] * x potatoes next year
   - We can plant at most b[i] potatoes in field i
4. We want to maximize the number of potatoes we have after one year of planting and harvesting

## Key Insights

1. Since we can only use fields 1 to K, we need to decide how to distribute our M potatoes among these K fields
2. The harvesting efficiency is determined by a[i] - higher a[i] means better return
3. We should prioritize fields with higher a[i] values because they give better return on investment
4. For each field, we can plant at most b[i] potatoes
5. We need to distribute M potatoes optimally across the K fields to maximize the total harvest

## Strategy

1. Since we want to maximize return, we should sort the fields by their a[i] values in descending order
2. For the K fields with highest a[i] values, we should distribute our M potatoes optimally
3. We should plant as many potatoes as possible in fields with higher a[i] values, but we can't exceed b[i] in any field
4. This becomes a simple greedy problem: sort by efficiency (a[i]) and distribute potatoes from highest to lowest efficiency

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Read input values
2. Extract the K fields with highest a[i] values (since we can only manage up to K fields)
3. Sort these K fields by a[i] in descending order
4. Distribute M potatoes greedily: for each field in order of efficiency, plant as many as possible (up to b[i] or remaining potatoes)
5. Calculate total harvest = sum of (planting amount in field i) * a[i]

## Implementation

```python
# Read input
N, M, K = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# We can only use fields 1 to K (indices 0 to K-1)
# We want to maximize harvest, so we should prioritize fields with higher a[i]

# Create list of fields we can use (first K fields)
fields = []
for i in range(K):
    fields.append((a[i], b[i]))

# Sort by efficiency (a[i]) in descending order
fields.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)

# Distribute potatoes greedily
remaining_potatoes = M
total_harvest = 0

for efficiency, max_planting in fields:
    # Plant as many as possible in this field
    to_plant = min(remaining_potatoes, max_planting)
    total_harvest += to_plant * efficiency
    remaining_potatoes -= to_plant
    
    # If no potatoes left, we're done
    if remaining_potatoes == 0:
        break

print(total_harvest)
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have N fields numbered 1 to N, and M potatoes to start with
2. We can only manage fields up to the Kth side (fields 1 to K)
3. For field i:
   - If we plant x potatoes, we get a_i × x potatoes harvested after one year
   - Maximum we can plant in field i is b_i potatoes
4. We want to maximize the total potatoes after one year

## Key Insights

1. Since we can only use fields 1 to K, we only consider the first K fields
2. The harvesting rate (a_i) determines how profitable each field is
3. We want to allocate our M potatoes optimally across the available fields to maximize total harvest
4. This is essentially a greedy problem: we should allocate more potatoes to fields with higher a_i values

## Strategy

1. Among fields 1 to K, sort them by their harvesting efficiency (a_i) in descending order
2. Allocate all M potatoes to the field with the highest a_i, then the next best, etc.
3. But we can't exceed the planting limit b_i for each field

## Step-by-step Solution

1. For fields 1 to K, we have harvesting rates a_i and planting limits b_i
2. We should prioritize fields with higher a_i values
3. We can use a greedy approach: sort fields by a_i descending, then allocate potatoes to each field up to its limit

## Implementation

```python
# Read input
N, M, K = map(int, input().split())
a = list(map(int, input().split()))
b = list(map(int, input().split()))

# We can only use fields 1 to K (indices 0 to K-1)
# Sort fields by harvesting rate (a_i) in descending order
fields = []
for i in range(K):
    fields.append((a[i], b[i]))

# Sort by harvesting rate descending
fields.sort(key=lambda x: x[0], reverse=True)

total_harvest = 0
potatoes_left = M

# Greedily allocate potatoes to fields with highest harvesting rate
for harvest_rate, plant_limit in fields:
    # Allocate as many potatoes as possible to this field
    # But don't exceed remaining potatoes or the planting limit
    to_plant = min(potatoes_left, plant_limit)
    total_harvest += to_plant * harvest_rate
    potatoes_left -= to_plant
    
    # If no potatoes left, we can stop
    if potatoes_left == 0:
        break

print(total_harvest)
```

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Snuke is visiting a shop in Tokyo called 109 to buy some logs.
He wants n logs: one of length 1, one of length 2, ..., and one of length n.
The shop has n+1 logs in stock: one of length 1, one of length 2, \dots, and one of length n+1. Each of these logs is sold for 1 yen (the currency of Japan).
He can cut these logs as many times as he wants after buying them. That is, he can get k logs of length L_1, \dots, L_k from a log of length L, where L = L_1 + \dots + L_k. He can also throw away unwanted logs.
Snuke wants to spend as little money as possible to get the logs he wants.
Find the minimum amount of money needed to get n logs of length 1 to n.

-----Constraints-----
 - 1 \leq n \leq 10^{18}

-----Input-----
Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
n

-----Output-----
Print the minimum amount of money needed to get n logs of length 1 to n.

-----Sample Input-----
4

-----Sample Output-----
3

One way to get the logs he wants with 3 yen is:
 - Buy logs of length 2, 4, and 5.
 - Cut the log of length 5 into two logs of length 1 each and a log of length 3.
 - Throw away one of the logs of length 1.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

## Problem Understanding

Snuke wants to get logs of lengths 1, 2, 3, ..., n. The shop sells logs of lengths 1, 2, 3, ..., n+1, each for 1 yen. He can cut any log into smaller pieces (or throw away any pieces he doesn't want).

We need to find the minimum amount of money needed to get exactly the logs of lengths 1 through n.

## Key Insights

1. If we buy a log of length L, we can get smaller logs by cutting it, but we can't create logs longer than L.

2. We need logs of lengths 1, 2, 3, ..., n. The log of length n+1 is particularly interesting because:
   - We can cut it into logs of lengths 1, 2, ..., n by proper cutting
   - We can get length 1, length 2, ..., length n from it

3. The question is whether we can get all the required logs without buying every individual log.

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Snuke is visiting a shop in Tokyo called 109 to buy some logs.
He wants n logs: one of length 1, one of length 2, ..., and one of length n.
The shop has n+1 logs in stock: one of length 1, one of length 2, \dots, and one of length n+1. Each of these logs is sold for 1 yen (the currency of Japan).
He can cut these logs as many times as he wants after buying them. That is, he can get k logs of length L_1, \dots, L_k from a log of length L, where L = L_1 + \dots + L_k. He can also throw away unwanted logs.
Snuke wants to spend as little money as possible to get the logs he wants.
Find the minimum amount of money needed to get n logs of length 1 to n.

-----Constraints-----
 - 1 \leq n \leq 10^{18}

-----Input-----
Input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
n

-----Output-----
Print the minimum amount of money needed to get n logs of length 1 to n.

-----Sample Input-----
4

-----Sample Output-----
3

One way to get the logs he wants with 3 yen is:
 - Buy logs of length 2, 4, and 5.
 - Cut the log of length 5 into two logs of length 1 each and a log of length 3.
 - Throw away one of the logs of length 1.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

- He can cut any log into smaller pieces (sum of lengths equals original length)

Snuke wants to spend as little money as possible to get the logs he wants.

Snuke is visiting a shop in Tokyo called 109 to buy some logs.

He can cut these logs as many times as he wants after buying them. That is, he can get k logs of length L_1, \dots, L_k from a log of length L, where L = L_1 + \dots + L_k. He can also throw away unwanted logs.
Snuke wants to spend as little money as possible to get the logs he wants.
Find the minimum amount of money needed to get n logs of length 1 to n.

n

One way to get the logs he wants with 3 yen is:

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

Snuke wants to buy logs of lengths 1, 2, 3, ..., n. The shop sells logs of lengths 1, 2, 3, ..., n+1, each for 1 yen.

He can cut logs into smaller pieces and throw away unwanted pieces. The goal is to minimize the total cost (number of logs bought).

## Key Insights

1. We need logs of lengths 1, 2, 3, ..., n
2. We can buy logs of lengths 1, 2, ..., n+1 (each costs 1 yen)
3. When we buy a log of length L, we can cut it into smaller pieces (sum to L)
4. We can throw away pieces we don't need

## Strategy Analysis

Let's think about what happens when we buy different logs:

- If we buy a log of length k (where k ≤ n), we can get pieces of various lengths
- The most efficient way is to consider whether we should buy the log of length n+1

Let me think about this differently:

The key insight is that we can always get a log of length k by:
- Buying a log of length k directly, or
- Buying a longer log and cutting it

However, when we cut a log, we lose the "value" of the unused parts.

But here's the crucial observation: if we buy a log of length n+1, we can cut it into pieces that sum to n+1. We need pieces of lengths 1, 2, ..., n.

The sum of lengths 1 to n is n(n+1)/2.

If we buy a log of length n+1, we can get a piece of length 1, a piece of length 2, ..., up to a piece of length n. But we need exactly one of each.

Actually, let me reconsider with the sample:
- n = 4, we need logs of lengths 1, 2, 3, 4
- The answer is 3
